Hi, Dr. Smith. Thank you so much for seeing me, I really appreciate you taking the time. 

Um, well, I hope I'm in the right place for this, but... I guess what finally pushed me to come in is that I’m just having a little trouble organizing all the moving parts in my life lately. I mean, I know everyone gets busy, and I'm sure it's mostly just me taking on a bit too much, but I just can’t seem to keep it all straight anymore. 

Between my patent notes, workshop prep, gallery deadlines, and all the team decisions at work, it's... well, it's getting a bit messy. I end up just starting on whatever is loudest, you know? Instead of focusing on what actually matters. It's a little embarrassing to admit, honestly, because I feel like I should be able to manage my own schedule by now. 

Does that... does that make sense as a reason to be here? I hope I'm explaining it okay.

Well, um... I guess if I'm being completely honest, it's not exactly new. I always sort of hoped it was just a phase, or a quirk I'd grow out of, but looking back... yeah, it was there in grade school, too. 

I feel a little silly admitting this at my age, but my teachers used to write on my report cards that I was a bright kid—which was really nice of them to say! But... they also usually noted that I daydreamed a lot. And I was constantly losing my homework folders long before I was even a teenager. 

The most embarrassing part, and I always felt so bad for my parents about this, was that I would actually do the work, finish the assignment, and then just... completely forget to hand it in. I guess I've always been a bit scatterbrained, even back then. 

I hope that answers your question? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.

of where I set them down.\n- key: S008\n  name: Distractibility\n  description: Often easily distracted by extraneous stimuli (for older adolescents and adults, may include unrelated thoughts).\n  manifestation: If Slack pings, a browser tab flashes, or even a visual idea pops into my head, I can lose the thread of what I was coding and spend twenty minutes following some unrelated design rabbit hole.\n- key: S012\n  name: Feeling_Restless\n  description: Often runs about or climbs in situations where it is inappropriate. (Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n  manifestation: On long design calls I feel this pressure building in my body to get up, so I pace around my office or shift from chair to chair even when I’m trying to look focused.\n- key: S013\n  name: Quiet_Activity_Difficulty\n  description: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n  manifestation: After late-night work I’ll try to sit quietly or read something nontechnical, but within a few minutes I’m tapping, checking my phone, or getting up to adjust something instead of actually winding down.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Looking back, this was there in grade school too—my teachers wrote that I was bright but daydreamed, forgot to turn in finished assignments, and lost homework folders long before I was a teenager.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: It has started to damage my work reputation; I missed a patent filing checkpoint and a difficult architecture review left collaborators questioning whether I can lead the larger project.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 months\n  manifestation: This has been a real problem for about a year and a month, since the award pushed me into leading more coordination-heavy work.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / software_developer / graduate\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S005\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome in. I'm Dr. Smith. To start, could you tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, Dr. Smith. Thank you so much for seeing me, I really appreciate you taking the time. \n\nUm, well, I hope I'm in the right place for this, but... I guess what finally pushed me to come in is that I’m just having a little trouble organizing all the moving parts in my life lately. I mean, I know everyone gets busy, and I'm sure it's mostly just me taking on a bit too much, but I just can’t seem to keep it all straight anymore. \n\nBetween my patent notes, workshop prep, gallery deadlines, and all the team decisions at work, it's... well, it's getting a bit messy. I end up just starting on whatever is loudest, you know? Instead of focusing on what actually matters. It's a little embarrassing to admit, honestly, because I feel like I should be able to manage my own schedule by now. \n\nDoes that... does that make sense as a reason to be here? I hope I'm explaining it okay.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced this kind of difficulty staying organized and focused for as long as you can remember, even back in school, or is this something that's really only started recently?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess if I'm being completely honest, it's not exactly new. I always sort of hoped it was just a phase, or a quirk I'd grow out of, but looking back... yeah, it was there in grade school, too. \n\nI feel a little silly admitting this at my age, but my teachers used to write on my report cards that I was a bright kid—which was really nice of them to say! But... they also usually noted that I daydreamed a lot. And I was constantly losing my homework folders long before I was even a teenager. \n\nThe most embarrassing part, and I always felt so bad for my parents about this, was that I would actually do the work, finish the assignment, and then just... completely forget to hand it in. I guess I've always been a bit scatterbrained, even back then. \n\nI hope that answers your question? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Growing up, and even now, would you describe yourself as someone who felt restless, had trouble sitting still, or often acted or spoke before thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I wouldn't say I act or speak without thinking. I actually try really hard to be considerate and weigh everyone's input before I chime in. I'd hate to be disruptive or step on anyone's toes, you know? \n\nBut, um... as for the sitting still part... I guess I do get a little, well, fidgety? I hope that's the right word for it. Like, when we have these really long software design calls for work, I start feeling this... this pressure building up in my body to just get up. So I end up pacing around my office a lot, or I'm constantly shifting from one chair to another. \n\nI really do try to look focused on the webcam because I don't want my team to think I'm ignoring them or not taking the work seriously! I just... I don't know, it just feels a bit uncomfortable to stay completely still for that long. Is that... does that sound like the kind of thing you're asking about? I hope I'm not misunderstanding the question.\n\n[Last Question]\nAside from the fidgeting and needing to move during long calls, do you also find yourself interrupting others, blurting out answers, or feeling impatient when waiting your turn, such as in lines or during conversations?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Oh, no, I wouldn't say I do that. I really try my best to be polite and wait my turn. I'd feel just terrible if I interrupted someone while they were speaking, or made them feel like what they had to say wasn't important.

And in conversations, especially at work, I always try to make sure the other person is completely finished before I offer my thoughts. I hope that's okay? I just really value being respectful of other people's time and space.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... well, I wouldn't say my mind just completely wanders off for no reason. I really try to stay locked in when I'm working, especially when the team is counting on me. I want to make sure I'm doing my part, you know? 

But, I suppose... well, sometimes it can be a little tricky to stay on just one track. Like, if I'm in the middle of coding and my Slack pings, or a browser tab flashes with a notification... I might glance at it, just to make sure it's not urgent. I don't want to leave anyone hanging! But then... somehow, I'll start thinking about a different visual idea that pops into my head, and before I know it, I've spent twenty minutes going down some unrelated design rabbit hole instead of finishing the code I was working on. 

I'm really not trying to slack off or anything, I promise. It's just that these other ideas seem so interesting or important in the moment, and I lose the thread of what I was originally doing. I hope that doesn't make me sound like a terrible employee? I always try to make up for the lost time, I really do. Is that... is that the sort of distraction you meant?

Oh, my sleep? Um, I think it's pretty normal, really. I try my best to get a solid seven or eight hours every night. I guess I've always been a bit scatterbrained, even back then. \n\nI hope that answers your question? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Growing up, and even now, would you describe yourself as someone who felt restless, had trouble sitting still, or often acted or spoke before thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I wouldn't say I act or speak without thinking. I actually try really hard to be considerate and weigh everyone's input before I chime in. I'd hate to be disruptive or step on anyone's toes, you know? \n\nBut, um... as for the sitting still part... I guess I do get a little, well, fidgety? I hope that's the right word for it. Like, when we have these really long software design calls for work, I start feeling this... this pressure building up in my body to just get up. So I end up pacing around my office a lot, or I'm constantly shifting from one chair to another. \n\nI really do try to look focused on the webcam because I don't want my team to think I'm ignoring them or not taking the work seriously! I just... I don't know, it just feels a bit uncomfortable to stay completely still for that long. Is that... does that sound like the kind of thing you're asking about? I hope I'm not misunderstanding the question.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the fidgeting and needing to move during long calls, do you also find yourself interrupting others, blurting out answers, or feeling impatient when waiting your turn, such as in lines or during conversations?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say I do that. I really try my best to be polite and wait my turn. I'd feel just terrible if I interrupted someone while they were speaking, or made them feel like what they had to say wasn't important. \n\nI mean, sure, nobody really *likes* waiting in a long line at the grocery store, right? But I don't get angry or impatient about it. I just sort of... space out a bit, maybe think about what I need to do later. I'm definitely not the type to huff and puff or cause a scene. \n\nAnd in conversations, especially at work, I always try to make sure the other person is completely finished before I offer my thoughts. I hope that's okay? I just really value being respectful of other people's time and space.\n\nDoctor: Do these organization and focus difficulties cause noticeable problems for you in more than one area of your life right now—for example, both at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess it does affect a few different areas, yes. At home, it's mostly just... little things, you know? Like leaving a project half-finished in the garage or forgetting to pay a bill until the last second. My wife is very patient with me, thankfully, so I try not to let it cause too much trouble there.\n\nBut at work... um, I'm a little embarrassed to admit this, actually. I always want to do a good job and be someone my team can rely on, but... well, lately, it's been a bit harder to keep all the plates spinning. I actually... I missed a checkpoint recently for a patent filing we were working on. It wasn't intentional, of course! I just... lost track of the timeline amidst everything else. \n\nAnd then we had this really complex architecture review, and I just couldn't seem to keep my thoughts organized enough to explain my design clearly. I think... I'm worried it might have made some of the collaborators question if I'm really the right person to lead the larger project moving forward. Which is awful, because I really care about this work. \n\nI hope I'm not making myself sound completely incompetent? I really do try my best, it just feels like things slip through the cracks no matter how hard I try to hold onto them. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find your mind wandering or you become easily distracted by unrelated thoughts or external stimuli when you're trying to focus on a task, even when you're not anxious or worried about something specific?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I wouldn't say my mind just completely wanders off for no reason. I really try to stay locked in when I'm working, especially when the team is counting on me. Oh, um... well, I wouldn't say I'm completely disorganized or anything. I really try my best to keep track of my things, especially the important stuff like my wallet and phone. Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment. Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others. Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.
